People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

And then I’ll sort of shake myself and think, “Why am I even thinking that?” It’s not something I’m proud of, and I wasn’t even sure whether to say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I probably shouldn’t just ignore it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I have. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts, because I’m usually pretty good at finding little things to keep myself level, you know? Like fishing — even if I didn’t catch anything, just being out there would settle me down. Or I’d spend way too long digging through old vinyl records, looking for some pressing or album I hadn’t heard in years. Tailgating too, that used to be fun for me, just the whole routine of it.

Lately, though, it’s like I don’t care. I’ll look at the fishing gear and think, “I should probably go,” but it feels like another errand. Same with checking on my home-brew — that used to be something I enjoyed fussing over, checking how it was coming along, but now it just feels like a chore I’m behind on. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the enjoyment got drained out of them, and I’m just going through the motions if I do them at all.

Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Not every single minute, I guess, but most of the day it’s there, like this heaviness I can’t quite shake.

thoughts were jumping so fast from sales numbers to job leads to beer recipes to old arguments that I couldn't keep hold of one idea for more than a few seconds.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n My usual routines at home got sloppy enough that I knew I could not just handle it by willpower.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 weeks\n  manifestation: That high-energy, out-of-control stretch lasted about three weeks after I was fired.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The low spell has been hanging on for about three months since that crash.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. And then I’ll sort of shake myself and think, “Why am I even thinking that?” It’s not something I’m proud of, and I wasn’t even sure whether to say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I probably shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. Or I’d spend way too long digging through old vinyl records, looking for some pressing or album I hadn’t heard in years. Tailgating too, that used to be fun for me, just the whole routine of it.\n\nLately, though, it’s like I don’t care. I’ll look at the fishing gear and think, “I should probably go,” but it feels like another errand. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the enjoyment got drained out of them, and I’m just going through the motions if I do them at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Not every single minute, I guess, but most of the day it’s there, like this heaviness I can’t quite shake.\n\nAfter that burst of energy I’d had before wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d just kind of collapse into myself. I remember sitting at the kitchen table with my coffee in front of me, just staring at it, not really drinking it, not really thinking anything useful. Just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is hard to explain because I couldn’t point to one thing and say, “That’s why.” It was more like this blanket of, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling down most days. Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.

I’ll sit there and look at something I probably should do, and I can’t quite tell whether I’m tired or just… stuck. So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s more like I look at the plate and just don’t have the interest in it. Even making something feels like more steps than it should be. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.

I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.

Like if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.

And as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.

I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.

thoughts were jumping so fast from sales numbers to job leads to beer recipes to old arguments that I couldn't keep hold of one idea for more than a few seconds.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: The only time I heard that store-manager voice or felt unreal certain I could take over any store was during those wired weeks after I got fired. Once I crashed into the low spell, the voice stopped and I was left embarrassed by what I had believed.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I fell behind on bills, missed callbacks and appointments, and could not keep a steady job search going even though I needed work. My usual routines at home got sloppy enough that I knew I could not just handle it by willpower.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 weeks\n  manifestation: That high-energy, out-of-control stretch lasted about three weeks after I was fired.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The low spell has been hanging on for about three months since that crash.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S032\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.

At first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.

I’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.

So… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.

I’d just kind of collapse into myself. I remember sitting at the kitchen table with my coffee in front of me, just staring at it, not really drinking it, not really thinking anything useful. Just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is hard to explain because I couldn’t point to one thing and say, “That’s why.” It was more like this blanket of, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling down most days. It’s not dramatic all the time, but it’s there, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been feeling more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you're not doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I don’t feel like I’m running around full of energy or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d call it tired in the usual sense. It’s not like after a long shift where your legs hurt and you just want to sit down.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been kind of slowed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy” or just me not wanting to start things. I’ll sit there and look at something I probably should do, and I can’t quite tell whether I’m tired or just… stuck. So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s more like I look at the plate and just don’t have the interest in it. Even making something feels like more steps than it should be. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. Like, I know I’ve had times before where I was down or didn’t really feel like doing much, but whether it was a full two weeks or more… I don’t know. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.

Sometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. I can remember being in a slump at different points, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that lasted at least two weeks and I lost interest in things the whole time.” It may have happened, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.

So I guess the best answer is I can’t really recall a definite time before this one. This current one is the one I’m more aware of because it’s been harder to ignore.

I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. Like, I know I’ve had times before where I was down or didn’t really feel like doing much, but whether it was a full two weeks or more… I don’t know. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nSometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. I can remember being in a slump at different points, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that lasted at least two weeks and I lost interest in things the whole time.” It may have happened, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is I can’t really recall a definite time before this one. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I’m trying not to dodge it, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that before this current stretch.

I can remember being down at different times in my life, sure. Like having a bad run, feeling worn out, not wanting to deal with people or do much for a while. But when you say two weeks or more, and losing interest in things in that same way, I can’t honestly say, yes, I remember that happening before. I just didn’t keep track of it like that, and looking back it all gets kind of fuzzy.

So… maybe it happened, I guess, but I can’t recall a definite period before this one where I know it lasted two weeks or longer. This one is the one that stands out because it’s been so steady and hard to shake.

I’ll look at the fishing gear and think, “I should probably go,” but it feels like another errand. It’s not dramatic all the time, but it’s there, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been feeling more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you're not doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I don’t feel like I’m running around full of energy or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d call it tired in the usual sense. It’s not like after a long shift where your legs hurt and you just want to sit down.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been kind of slowed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy” or just me not wanting to start things. I’ll sit there and look at something I probably should do, and I can’t quite tell whether I’m tired or just… stuck. So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. I hear what you’re asking, and I know I’ve kind of been circling around it, but I really don’t know that I can give you a definite yes.

It’s not dramatic all the time, but it’s there, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been feeling more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you're not doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I don’t feel like I’m running around full of energy or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d call it tired in the usual sense. It’s not like after a long shift where your legs hurt and you just want to sit down.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been kind of slowed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy” or just me not wanting to start things. I’ll sit there and look at something I probably should do, and I can’t quite tell whether I’m tired or just… stuck. So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s more like I look at the plate and just don’t have the interest in it. Even making something feels like more steps than it should be. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nSometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. I can remember being in a slump at different points, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that lasted at least two weeks and I lost interest in things the whole time.” It may have happened, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is I can’t really recall a definite time before this one. This current one is the one I’m more aware of because it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I’m trying not to dodge it, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that before this current stretch.\n\nI can remember being down at different times in my life, sure. Yeah… that wording actually fits better than just asking if I was “sad,” because it wasn’t always like crying or anything obvious like that.

After that burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody had pulled the plug on me. Like one day I’d been running on something, and then suddenly there was just nothing there. I remember sitting at the kitchen table with my coffee, not even drinking it half the time, just staring at it. And I’d feel this emptiness, but also ashamed, like, “What did I do? Why can’t I just get up and act normal?”

So yes, I guess there were times where I was just kind of moving through the day because I had to. I’d push myself, but it felt flat, like I wasn’t really in it. It’s hard for me to say exactly how many weeks, but that “going through the motions” feeling, yeah, I know what you mean.

So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s more like I look at the plate and just don’t have the interest in it. Even making something feels like more steps than it should be. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. Like, I know I’ve had times before where I was down or didn’t really feel like doing much, but whether it was a full two weeks or more… I don’t know. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nSometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. This current one is the one I’m more aware of because it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I’m trying not to dodge it, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that before this current stretch.\n\nI can remember being down at different times in my life, sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More than one time… I’m trying to sort that out. I know there was at least that kind of crash after I’d been all wound up or full of energy, and then it was like somebody just pulled the plug on me. That’s the clearest one I can remember.

I’d sit at the table with my coffee in front of me and just stare at it, not really doing anything, feeling empty and kind of ashamed of myself. Like I should be able to just get up and act like a normal person, but I couldn’t get myself there.

As far as whether that happened more than once before now, nearly every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that with confidence. There may have been other times that felt similar, but that one is the one I can really picture. I don’t want to make it sound more certain than it is.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main reason I came in is because I’ve been having these thoughts that worry me. And then I’ll sort of shake myself and think, “Why am I even thinking that?” It’s not something I’m proud of, and I wasn’t even sure whether to say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I probably shouldn’t just ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. That’s actually been one of the stranger parts, because I’m usually pretty good at finding little things to keep myself level, you know? Like fishing — even if I didn’t catch anything, just being out there would settle me down. Or I’d spend way too long digging through old vinyl records, looking for some pressing or album I hadn’t heard in years. Tailgating too, that used to be fun for me, just the whole routine of it.\n\nLately, though, it’s like I don’t care. I’ll look at the fishing gear and think, “I should probably go,” but it feels like another errand. Same with checking on my home-brew — that used to be something I enjoyed fussing over, checking how it was coming along, but now it just feels like a chore I’m behind on. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the enjoyment got drained out of them, and I’m just going through the motions if I do them at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s fair to say. Not every single minute, I guess, but most of the day it’s there, like this heaviness I can’t quite shake.\n\nAfter that burst of energy I’d had before wore off, it was like somebody pulled the plug on me. I’d just kind of collapse into myself. I remember sitting at the kitchen table with my coffee in front of me, just staring at it, not really drinking it, not really thinking anything useful. Just feeling empty. And ashamed, too, which is hard to explain because I couldn’t point to one thing and say, “That’s why.” It was more like this blanket of, “What is wrong with me?”\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling down most days. It’s not dramatic all the time, but it’s there, and it wears on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been feeling more tired or that you have less energy than usual, even if you're not doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean… I don’t feel like I’m running around full of energy or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d call it tired in the usual sense. It’s not like after a long shift where your legs hurt and you just want to sit down.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been kind of slowed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy” or just me not wanting to start things. I’ll sit there and look at something I probably should do, and I can’t quite tell whether I’m tired or just… stuck. So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s more like I look at the plate and just don’t have the interest in it. Even making something feels like more steps than it should be. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. Like, I know I’ve had times before where I was down or didn’t really feel like doing much, but whether it was a full two weeks or more… I don’t know. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nSometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. I can remember being in a slump at different points, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that lasted at least two weeks and I lost interest in things the whole time.” It may have happened, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is I can’t really recall a definite time before this one. This current one is the one I’m more aware of because it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I’m trying not to dodge it, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that before this current stretch.\n\nI can remember being down at different times in my life, sure. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. Like, I know I’ve had times before where I was down or didn’t really feel like doing much, but whether it was a full two weeks or more… I don’t know. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nSometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. I can remember being in a slump at different points, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that lasted at least two weeks and I lost interest in things the whole time.” It may have happened, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is I can’t really recall a definite time before this one. This current one is the one I’m more aware of because it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I’m trying not to dodge it, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that before this current stretch.\n\nI can remember being down at different times in my life, sure. Like having a bad run, feeling worn out, not wanting to deal with people or do much for a while. But when you say two weeks or more, and losing interest in things in that same way, I can’t honestly say, yes, I remember that happening before. I just didn’t keep track of it like that, and looking back it all gets kind of fuzzy.\n\nSo… maybe it happened, I guess, but I can’t recall a definite period before this one where I know it lasted two weeks or longer. Like one day I’d been running on something, and then suddenly there was just nothing there. I know I already mentioned it, but the one that really stands out is after that burst of energy wore off. It was like I’d been running on something, and then all of a sudden it was gone—like someone pulled the plug out of the wall.

I’d sit at the table with my coffee in front of me and just… stare at it. Not really drinking it, not really thinking anything useful, just feeling empty. And embarrassed, honestly. Ashamed, like I should have been able to get up and be normal, go do what I needed to do, but I couldn’t seem to find the inside part of me that makes that happen.

As far as more than one time before this current one… I don’t know that I can clearly separate them out. That crash is the one I can really picture. There may have been other stretches that were similar, but I don’t want to say for sure there was more than one if I can’t pin it down.

I mean… I don’t feel like I’m running around full of energy or anything, but I also don’t know if I’d call it tired in the usual sense. It’s not like after a long shift where your legs hurt and you just want to sit down.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been kind of slowed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “less energy” or just me not wanting to start things. I’ll sit there and look at something I probably should do, and I can’t quite tell whether I’m tired or just… stuck. So, yeah, maybe a little, but I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s definitely been off. Most days lately I have to remind myself to eat, which is not really like me. I’ll get to the end of the day and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and something small, and then supper is just sitting there and it looks like this whole task, like too much trouble to deal with.\n\nIt’s not that the food tastes bad, exactly. It’s more like I look at the plate and just don’t have the interest in it. Even making something feels like more steps than it should be. And my jeans have been fitting looser, so I’m guessing I’ve lost some weight, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything. I just notice I’m pulling my belt a little tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you're not good enough, or blamed yourself for things more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it exactly like “not good enough.” I mean, I have moments where I’m hard on myself, sure, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or if it’s just… I’m noticing it more because I’m already not feeling great.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there and think, “You should be handling things better,” or “Why can’t you just get yourself together?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as really blaming myself for everything. It’s not like I have one specific thing I keep replaying over and over, at least not that I can clearly point to. It’s more of a general frustration with myself, I guess.\n\nSo… maybe a little, but I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say it’s been the main thing I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed anything obvious like that. I mean, I’m probably not moving around as much as I usually do, but I can’t tell if that’s moving slowly exactly, or just me taking longer to get myself going.\n\nLike if I’m getting ready to go somewhere, I might sit on the edge of the bed for a bit or stand in the kitchen and sort of think through what I’m supposed to be doing next, but I don’t know if someone watching me would say, “He’s slowed down.” Nobody’s really said that to me, at least.\n\nAnd as far as not being able to sit still, no, not really. I’m not pacing around or feeling like I have to keep moving. If anything, I can sit too long and then realize I’ve just been sitting there. So… I guess I’d say I’m not sure about being slower, and I haven’t really felt restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're having more trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say there’s been some big obvious thing where I can’t think straight, exactly. I have moments where I feel kind of foggy, I guess, but then again I don’t know if that’s just normal tiredness or being preoccupied.\n\nAt work, I can still do what I need to do for the most part. Maybe I’ll stand there a second longer trying to remember what I was about to grab or why I walked over to a shelf, but that happens to everybody, right? And decisions… I don’t know. Little decisions can feel annoying, like what to eat or what to do first, but I don’t know if I’d call it trouble making decisions in a serious way.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a major change. It’s more like I feel a bit off and slower to get started, but I can’t say my concentration is dramatically worse or anything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — this heaviness, loss of interest, and other changes you’ve described?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about three months now. More or less since that crash, when things just kind of dropped off for me.\n\nAt first I thought it was just a bad week, you know, like I’d bounce back after a little time. Then it became a couple weeks, and I kept telling myself I was just tired or needed to get through it. But looking back, it’s been hanging around pretty steadily for about three months. It hasn’t felt like just a day or two here and there. It’s more like this low spell has been sitting on me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods like this before — where you've felt this way for weeks at a time — or is this the first time you've experienced something like this?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone does, where I’m down for a bit or just kind of tired of everything for a few days, maybe even longer than I’d like. But whether it was like this, where it goes on for weeks and has that same heavy feeling… I don’t know.\n\nI’m trying to think back, and it’s hard because when you’re out of it, you don’t always mark the dates in your head. There have been times in my life where I wasn’t myself, I guess, but I can’t clearly say, “Yes, that was the same thing and it lasted this many weeks.” This one feels more noticeable to me because it’s been hanging around, but I don’t know if it’s the first time ever or just the first time I’ve really paid attention to it this way.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of that clearly, and I honestly can’t say for sure. Like, I know I’ve had times before where I was down or didn’t really feel like doing much, but whether it was a full two weeks or more… I don’t know. I didn’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nSometimes when you look back, everything kind of blurs together. I can remember being in a slump at different points, but I can’t confidently say, “Yes, that lasted at least two weeks and I lost interest in things the whole time.” It may have happened, but I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is I can’t really recall a definite time before this one. This current one is the one I’m more aware of because it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back and recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things for a period of two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I’m trying not to dodge it, but I really don’t have a clear memory of that before this current stretch.\n\nI can remember being down at different times in my life, sure. Like having a bad run, feeling worn out, not wanting to deal with people or do much for a while. But when you say two weeks or more, and losing interest in things in that same way, I can’t honestly say, yes, I remember that happening before. I just didn’t keep track of it like that, and looking back it all gets kind of fuzzy.\n\nSo… maybe it happened, I guess, but I can’t recall a definite period before this one where I know it lasted two weeks or longer. This one is the one that stands out because it’s been so steady and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life before this current one when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I hear what you’re asking, and I know I’ve kind of been circling around it, but I really don’t know that I can give you a definite yes.\n\nBefore this current time, I can remember being low here and there, or getting into periods where I didn’t feel like myself, but I can’t honestly look back and say, “That was nearly every day for two weeks or more.” I just didn’t keep track of it that way, and now when I try to sort it out in my head, it’s not clear enough.\n\nSo I’d say… I’m not sure. I don’t have a specific earlier period I can point to and say for certain that it was like that.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was ever a time before this current period when you had to really push yourself to get through the day, or felt like you were just going through the motions for weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that wording actually fits better than just asking if I was “sad,” because it wasn’t always like crying or anything obvious like that.\n\nAfter that burst of energy wore off, it really felt like somebody had pulled the plug on me. Like one day I’d been running on something, and then suddenly there was just nothing there. I remember sitting at the kitchen table with my coffee, not even drinking it half the time, just staring at it. And I’d feel this emptiness, but also ashamed, like, “What did I do? Why can’t I just get up and act normal?”\n\nSo yes, I guess there were times where I was just kind of moving through the day because I had to. I’d push myself, but it felt flat, like I wasn’t really in it. It’s hard for me to say exactly how many weeks, but that “going through the motions” feeling, yeah, I know what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you have had more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: More than one time… I’m trying to sort that out. I know there was at least that kind of crash after I’d been all wound up or full of energy, and then it was like somebody just pulled the plug on me. That’s the clearest one I can remember.\n\nI’d sit at the table with my coffee in front of me and just stare at it, not really doing anything, feeling empty and kind of ashamed of myself. Like I should be able to just get up and act like a normal person, but I couldn’t get myself there.\n\nAs far as whether that happened more than once before now, nearly every day for two weeks or more… I don’t know if I can say that with confidence. There may have been other times that felt similar, but that one is the one I can really picture. I don’t want to make it sound more certain than it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you have had more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know I already mentioned it, but the one that really stands out is after that burst of energy wore off. It was like I’d been running on something, and then all of a sudden it was gone—like someone pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d sit at the table with my coffee in front of me and just… stare at it. Not really drinking it, not really thinking anything useful, just feeling empty. And embarrassed, honestly. That crash is the one I can really picture. I know I’ve kind of circled around this already, but the clearest time I can remember is after that burst of energy wore off. It was like somebody had pulled the plug on me. I don’t mean just having a bad day or being tired after work — it felt more like the inside of me went flat.

As far as whether it happened more than once, I’m not sure I can separate it out cleanly. That one stretch is the one I can really picture. There may have been other times that were like that, where I was just dragging through and not interested in anything, but I don’t want to say yes for sure if I can’t really remember it clearly.

I don’t want to make it sound more certain than it is.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you have had more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know I already mentioned it, but the one that really stands out is after that burst of energy wore off. It was like I’d been running on something, and then all of a sudden it was gone—like someone pulled the plug out of the wall.\n\nI’d sit at the table with my coffee in front of me and just… stare at it. Not really drinking it, not really thinking anything useful, just feeling empty. And embarrassed, honestly. Ashamed, like I should have been able to get up and be normal, go do what I needed to do, but I couldn’t seem to find the inside part of me that makes that happen.\n\nAs far as more than one time before this current one… I don’t know that I can clearly separate them out. That crash is the one I can really picture. There may have been other stretches that were similar, but I don’t want to say for sure there was more than one if I can’t pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you have had more than one time in your life before this current period when you felt down or lost interest in things nearly every day for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve kind of circled around this already, but the clearest time I can remember is after that burst of energy wore off. It was like somebody had pulled the plug on me. I don’t mean just having a bad day or being tired after work — it felt more like the inside of me went flat.\n\nI’d sit at the kitchen table with my coffee there, and I’d just stare at it. Sometimes it would get cold before I even took more than a sip. And I remember feeling empty, but also ashamed, like I was looking at myself from the outside thinking, “Come on, what is wrong with you? Just get up. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know I’ve said pieces of this already, but the clearest thing I can remember is after that burst of energy wore off. It was like somebody had pulled the plug on me. Not just “I’m tired” or “I’m having a rough week,” but like everything inside just went flat.

I’d sit at the table with my coffee in front of me and just stare at it. I remember that pretty clearly. I’d feel empty, and then ashamed for feeling that way, like I should’ve been able to just get up and do something normal. But I’d just sit there, stuck.

As far as whether that happened more than one separate time before now, I’m not completely sure. That one stretch is the one I can really picture. There may have been other times like it, but I don’t want to say yes for sure if I can’t honestly separate them out in my memory.
